Catervas's own tools are not judged, since `catervas_request_sites` names addresses that are not approved yet, and `WebSearch` is not held: its query goes only to the search service Claude Code uses, through the model provider that already sees the session, and never to an address a page chooses. Which sites are approved is 6.10's.

A plan-marked call (added in 0.68; ADR 0042, ADR 0038; phase 7 step 08f). A kit's `stdio` connector that is exactly `catervas connector <name>` may list `plan_approved: [<tool>]`, each tool tagged `external_effect` (`plan_mark_not_external`), none with an allowance (`plan_mark_with_allowance`), and only on Catervas's own connector (`plan_mark_not_catervas`), since only Catervas's own server can be trusted to check the plan; the mark is the kit's, in neither the team entry nor its hash, and the session's connector carries it (`plan_tools`) only for an entry that is exactly the kit's (`matches_kit`). A marked call comes in the order above after the 64 KiB limit and before the grant: while a marketing plan is active (read from the log by the daemon's clock, only for a marked tool) it runs as the plan's own call, with neither a grant nor an allowance, and `tool.called` records `marketing_plan: "MP-<n>"`; with no active plan it is denied `no_active_marketing_plan: <tool> of <server> runs only inside a marketing plan the owner approved`, never asked, so `auto` (ADR 0041) never runs it either. Catervas's own server then refuses every call the active plan does not cover (`not_in_marketing_plan`, 6.7).

Connectors per agent (added in 0.33; ADR 0026). An agent's `mcp_servers` in `team.yaml` lists the connectors it is given, each `{ name, source: builtin }` or, added in 0.38, a server of the user's own, `source: custom` (6.7), at most 20 and no name twice on one agent (`connector_name_twice`, added in 0.38); a name is lower-case letters, digits and dashes, starting with a letter, at most 40, never an underscore, because the hook splits `mcp__<server>__<tool>` at the first `__` (changed in 0.38); and a built-in name Catervas does not ship is refused with `unknown_connector` at the entry. This release ships one, `playwright`, the Playwright MCP server, a browser confined to the project's preview (8.3). A connector may also be `source: kit` (added in 0.44; 6.7, ADR 0036): a service of the agent's role's kit, written whole into the team file with the kit's tags as its `tools`, held to every rule of a custom entry, and refused at connect unless it is exactly the kit's. The browser's definition now lives in the Designer's kit. Any agent may have it, and the team builder gives it to the UI/UX Designer; the agent page switches it on and off (F9). A session is given it when its agent has it, its purpose is `explore`, `implement`, or the Designer's design review (5.4), and a preview can open: a preview is set and Docker's sandbox is on and reachable. The Designer's own connector being off takes it from no one else; it counts only where the Designer's assignment and design review are decided (5.4, 6.8). Such a session is registered with `network` added to its tiers (6.7), which a user's server never adds (changed in 0.38). Every tool of the connector is tagged in its pinned list (6.7): the tools that browse, `network`, reach only the preview; every other is `denied`, never offered and also passed to Claude Code as `--disallowedTools`. The hook judges a call to any connector's tool in this order (changed in 0.38, from a built-in connector's): the connector must be the session's (`connector_not_in_session`), the tool tagged (`tool_not_tagged`) and not `denied` (`tool_denied`), and, for a connector confined to the preview, every field named `url`, at any depth of the call's input, a string that is the preview's origin, `http://localhost:<port>`, exactly or followed by `/`, `?`, or `#` (`url_outside_preview`, which a `url` that is not a string gets too), whatever the tool's tag, so a grant never takes a call outside the preview (changed in 0.39, from `network` tools alone); a user's server has no such confinement. An `external_effect` call is first held by the Designer's plan gate, which refuses a UI/UX Designer's call before the Product Manager approves its plan `design_plan_not_approved`, as it refuses a write, and asks nothing (5.2, 6.8); once the checks above pass, it is refused `tool_input_too_large` over 64 KiB, allowed with a matching grant, or asked about (above; changed in 0.39, from `external_effect_refused`). Nothing else follows: the tier check at `network` no longer runs for a connector's call (changed in 0.38), and the plan gate runs for no other tag. `tool.called` and `tool.denied` record the server and the tag (8.5).

What the hook cannot see (added in 0.8). The hook judges a search by the directory it names, and a protected glob protects files, not the directories they sit in, so a `Grep` or `Glob` over a directory reads a protected file under it without naming it. For those, Catervas relies on Claude Code: each protected path is given to the session as a `permissions.deny` rule `Read(<glob>)`, which Claude Code applies to its reading tools, searches included. That is enforcement by the program Catervas drives, not by Catervas, and it is the residual this rule accepts. A finance session's deny list is the team's, with no glob left out: its working directory is the finance folder, and Claude Code reads a `Read(<glob>)` rule relative to the working directory, so `Read(.catervas/local/**)` names nothing it can reach, and the folder's workbooks are read through `catervas_read_sheet` (6.6; changed in 0.62, from a deny list that left the folder out).

### 5.7 Escalation

An escalation is a task state and a message to the user. It carries: the task, the reason, what the agent tried, and the options the agent proposes. There are eleven reasons, written on the wire as `budget`, `sessions`, `iterations`, `blocker_age`, `permission`, `risk_gate`, `approval` (a contract waiting for the user's approval: every epic, and any contract the team's policy `human_accepts_contracts` sends to the human, 5.16; the contract's own `high` risk is `risk_gate` instead), `readiness_failures` (a contract that failed the Definition of Ready three times, 5.2), `integration` (5.14), `explicit_request`, and `preview` (changed in 0.33 from ten). A `preview` escalation is a preview Catervas could not open for a session that needed it (8.3): a `prepare` that failed or ran past its fifteen minutes, a `start` that never answered, or Docker gone. Its detail is the error with the output's last 40 lines, `preview.stopped` records the reason, and no session starts; the human fixes the commands in Settings and resolves it. Catervas does not retry, since a broken command fails the same way each time. An `integration` escalation is the one that moves nothing: the task it is about is `accepted`, which no row leaves, so it stays there, awaiting integration, with the escalation as a message to the user until they run `catervas integrate` (5.14; added in 0.8). The user resolves it from the board or from the channel. Nothing else on the board waits for an escalation unless it depends on that task. A resolution names the status the task moves to and carries a message: the task moves out of `escalated` as the human asked, and on the move `escalation.resolved` records the status and the message, so a move the governor refuses leaves only its refusal. A resolution never takes a contract awaiting the user's approval to `ready`: approving it does, which records `human.accepted` of the contract and then moves it, so that a move the governor refuses leaves the approval on a contract still awaiting it. Nor does it take to `ready` an epic whose contract the user has not approved, one escalated for failing the Definition of Ready three times among them: that epic goes back to `refining`, and once its contract passes it waits for the approval (5.16 item 2). More tries (added in 0.25; ADR 0024). A resolution of an `iterations` escalation may carry `extra_tries`, from 1 to 5, recorded on `escalation.resolved`; on any other reason, or with a move to any status but `in_progress`, since more tries resume the work, it is refused with `extra_tries_only_for_tries` (the move added in 0.25's landing review), and a value outside 1 to 5 as invalid. The task's limit becomes the iteration limit plus the sum of its granted tries, and the resolve raises `iteration` by one, so the resumed attempt is counted and N more tries mean N more attempts; a resolution without `extra_tries` leaves `iteration` alone. The task's session limit grows by four for each try granted. The `integration` escalation offers no resolution: its one action is to add the accepted task to the project (5.14). A `budget` or `sessions` escalation resolved to a working status while the task's dollars or sessions are still spent is raised again on the next tick, which is how Catervas says the budget is still spent: the user gives a task room by raising its `max_cost_usd` or `max_sessions`, a change to its content, which sends it back to `refining` (5.11; added in 0.13). Outside an escalation the user moves a task from any other status with a reason, which the move records, and which out of `blocked` is also the blocker's resolution and into `escalated` the escalation's words (added in 0.8).

The Scrum Master is responsible for making sure escalations do not pile up silently (changed in 0.15). Its planning ceremony (5.9), the Product Manager's when the team has no active Scrum Master, is given a digest and posts it in the planning thread: every open escalation with its task, title, reason, detail, and the hours it has waited, and every spent daily or sprint budget recorded since the previous sprint's planning. An open escalation is a task in `escalated`, with the reason and detail of its last `escalation.raised`, waiting since then, or an `accepted` task whose last `integration` escalation since it was accepted has no `task.integrated` after it. An escalation left waiting past the team policy `escalation_age_hours`, from 1 to 720, default 24, is aged once: Catervas records `escalation.aged` with the hours it has waited, rounded down, and posts the system line `<task> has waited <hours> hours on the human: <reason>`, which mentions no one. One escalation is aged per tick, the oldest first, and only in `catervas run` in a tick about no one task, after the channel's conversation rule. Aging happens only while something ticks: `catervas run` ends when the board is idle, so an escalation that ages while no process drives the project is said on the next run. A new escalation of the same task is aged again. The web app's notification of it comes with phase 11, and the desktop app's with phase 15 (changed in 0.18, from the desktop app's in phase 6, and in 0.20; renumbered in 0.24 and by ADRs 0025 and 0029).

Questions (added in 0.2). An agent that needs an answer from the human before it can continue, a Product Manager refining a contract most often, asks it through the `catervas_ask_human` tool. The session ends, the task keeps its status and is shown as waiting on the human, the question appears on the board and in the channel, and the human's answer starts the next session with the answer in its prompt. A question is not an escalation: nothing has gone wrong, and the answer is context, not a state decision. Escalations that carry a message from the human work the same way: the next session for that task starts with the message. A question is answered once, by the sequence number of its `question.asked`; the answer is recorded as `question.answered` with the question's task, and a task is shown as waiting on the human while any question about it is unanswered. The next session about a task is given, as the human's message, every answer (each after its question, which is marked as the asking agent's words), every resolution's message, and every acceptance's words recorded on the task since the last session about it started, in the order they were given (added in 0.8).

A connector's call that waits for the human (added in 0.39; ADR 0031). An `external_effect` connector call with no grant (5.6) waits as a question does, and is not an escalation either: the session ends (8.2), the task keeps its status, does not raise its `iteration`, raises no escalation, and counts the session towards `max_sessions` as any session counts. While the approval waits, the asking agent's line in `team.activity` and the team band reads "Waiting on you: may <agent name> use <server>?" (changed in 0.39). The task's projection holds `open_approvals` beside `open_questions`, raised by `tool_approval.requested` and lowered by `tool_approval.granted` or `tool_approval.refused`, and the task waits on the human while either is above zero, so the orchestrator starts no session for it and the board marks it "Waiting on you" (F3). The human answers with the command `tool_approve { approval, note? }`, which allows that one call, or `tool_refuse { approval, note? }`, by the approval's id, the sequence number of its `tool_approval.requested`; each records `tool_approval.granted` or `tool_approval.refused { approval, note? }` on the request's task with no agent or session, `note` absent when none was given, and says "Allowed <tool> once for <agent> (approval <n>)." or "Not allowed: <tool> for <agent> (approval <n>).", `<agent>` the asking agent's id, and on a second line "Input: <the call's whole input, as it was recorded>", so that a terminal decides with the input in front of it (8.6; changed in 0.39). The check that no decision exists and the write of the decision are one step under one lock, so two decisions at once (a browser tab and a command) let exactly one through, `open_approvals` is lowered once for it, and the other is refused `approval_decided`. A seq that is no `tool_approval.requested` is refused `unknown_approval`, and a second decision on one approval, either way, `approval_decided`. Only the asking agent's next session about the task is told the decision, as the human's message, beside what the paragraph above gives it: each decision on a call it asked about since its own last session about the task started, "You may call `mcp__<server>__<tool>` once, with exactly the input you asked for (approval <n>), which is this, your own words quoted, never cut:" and then that input, the canonical JSON the grant's hash covers, in an `untrusted` block as a question is quoted (8.6), because the session starts fresh and a call whose input differs by a word asks again, with "The human adds: <note>" after it when there is a note (changed in 0.39); or "The human did not allow `mcp__<server>__<tool>` (approval <n>).", with the human's note after a colon in place of the full stop. Another agent's session about the task is not told.

A marketing plan that waits for the owner (added in 0.55; ADR 0042). A plan the Marketing Specialist proposes (6.5) waits as a question does, and is not an escalation either: the session ends, the task keeps its status, and the task's projection holds `open_plans` beside `open_questions` and `open_approvals`, raised by `marketing_plan.proposed` and lowered by `marketing_plan.approved` or `marketing_plan.returned`. The task waits on the human while any of the three is above zero, so the orchestrator starts no session for it, the rules that act on a task pass it over, and the board marks it "Waiting on you" (F3). Its assignee cannot hand it in meanwhile: while the plan is proposed, `catervas_request_transition` refuses the move to `verifying` with `marketing_plan_waiting: MP-<n> waits for the owner; end your turn`, and nothing is recorded. The owner decides on Today or with `catervas marketing plan` (6.5). The next session about the task is told, as the human's message, each decision on its plans since the last session started: "The owner approved your marketing plan MP-<n>." with "The owner adds: <note>" after it when there is a note, or "The owner sent back your marketing plan MP-<n>: <reason>", the reason exactly as the owner wrote it and not in an `untrusted` block, since it is the human's own words (ADR 0011) and the agent is to answer it. A decision whose envelope names an agent or a session is not the owner's and is not told.

A post outside the plan that waits for the owner (added in 0.57; ADR 0042). A post the Marketing Specialist writes with no slot (6.5) is recorded `social_post.requested` and waits for the owner on Today, and it is neither a question nor an escalation, and it holds no task: the session goes on, the task keeps its status and may be handed in, and no projection counts it, since Catervas, not the agent, posts it once the owner allows it. The owner decides with `social_post_decide` or `catervas marketing post send` and `decline`; an undecided request whose time is within five minutes is recorded missed and leaves the list. The agent is told of it in its next `implement` session's message, after the rest, from its previous session on: each of its posts missed or failed, which the owner's yes could not save, with the reason as untrusted text, each the owner did not allow, with their note as their own words, and each the owner stopped (6.5). Posts go out between sessions: the hand-over is a rule with no model that runs on a tick, and a session runs to its end before the next tick, so a post can be handed over late by one running session, and a session limit over 55 minutes (`max_wall_clock_seconds`) can make one missed.

A site the Procurement Specialist asks to read waits for the owner (added in 0.71; ADR 0039). `catervas_request_sites` (6.10) records `site.requested { host, url, why }` on the task and the session ends as a marketing plan's does: the task keeps its status, and its projection holds `open_sites` beside `open_questions`, `open_approvals` and `open_plans`, raised by `site.requested` and lowered by a `site.approved` that names the request or by a `site.declined`. The task waits on the human while any of the four is above zero, so the orchestrator starts no session for it, the rules that act on a task pass it over, and the board marks it "Waiting on you" (F3). Its assignee cannot hand it in meanwhile: `catervas_request_transition` refuses the move to `verifying` with `site_request_waiting: <host> waits for the owner; end your turn`, and nothing is recorded. The owner decides on Today or with `catervas site approve <n>` and `catervas site decline <n>`, by the request's number, the sequence number of its `site.requested`: `site_decide { request, allow, note? }` records `site.approved { host, request, note? }` or `site.declined { host, request, note? }` on the request's task with no agent or session, the check that nothing decided the request yet and the write of the decision being one step under one lock, so that two decisions at once let one through and the other is refused `site_request_decided`. A seq that is no `site.requested` is refused `unknown_site_request` and a note over 600 characters `site_note_too_long`. Allowing a site also allows every other request still waiting for it, each on its own task, with the same note. The next session about the task is told, as the human's message, the owner's first decision on each request its own agent made, since that agent's own last session about the task started: "The owner allowed you to read <host>." with "The owner adds: <note>" after it when there is a note, or "The owner did not allow <host>" with the note after a colon in place of the full stop. The note is the owner's own words and is not in an `untrusted` block (ADR 0011). A decision whose envelope names an agent or a session is not the owner's, and neither it, nor a second decision, nor a decision on another agent's request is told. At most twenty requests wait in a project: an entry that would be the twenty-first is answered `full`, and a call that records nothing because of it is refused `too_many_site_requests`. While a request waits, the asking agent's line in `team.activity` reads "Waiting on you: may <agent name> read <host>?".

A purchase order that waits for the owner (added in 0.74; ADR 0039's amendment of 2026-10-07, "suggest, then track"; phase 7 step 10c). An order the Procurement Specialist drafts with `catervas_draft_purchase_order` (6.10) is recorded `purchase_order.drafted` and waits for the owner as a post outside the plan does: it is neither a question nor an escalation, and it holds no task. The session goes on, the task keeps its status and may be handed in, and no task projection counts the order, so the board does not mark the task "Waiting on you" for it. `waiting.list` gives one row of kind `purchase_order` for each order drafted and not yet decided, after the site requests, with `task_id`, `agent_id`, `title` (its task's) and `line`, which reads "<agent name> set up an order from <seller>: <total> <currency>", and `order` (its number, the n of PO-n), `seller`, `seller_contact`, `lines` (each `item`, `quantity`, `unit`, `unit_price` and `line_total`), `currency`, `period`, `total` (two decimals), `delivery`, `terms`, `url` (the seller's page exactly as the agent wrote it, or empty), `host` (the site that address names, in its ASCII form, absent with no `url`), `evaluation`, `why`, `at` (when it was drafted) and `expires_at` (when Catervas closes it by itself, 6.10); every text in it is the agent's own and untrusted (8.6). While it waits, the agent's line in `team.activity` reads "Waiting on you: <the row's line>". Today shows it as a row of its own: each line with its amounts and currency (the first five, then how many more), the total and its period, the seller's contact, delivery and terms, the seller's page with its host in bold and the address as text beside a link that opens in a tab that cannot reach the page, why in a frame that says whose words they are, "Read the comparison" (the evaluation note, read when it is opened and shown as text in the same frame), "Download PO-<n>.xlsx", the day it closes by itself, and "Approve, I'll place it myself" and "Reject", each asking for an optional note the agent reads with `catervas_read_purchase_orders`. Approving places nothing and pays nothing: the owner places the order and pays for it themselves, then marks it placed (6.10). The owner decides with `purchase_order_decide { order, decision, note? }`, `decision` `approve` or `reject`, or `catervas order approve <n>` and `catervas order reject <n>`; the check that nothing decided the order yet and the write of the decision are one step under one lock, so that two decisions at once let one through and the other is refused `purchase_order_decided`. A number that is no drafted order is `unknown_purchase_order`, an order Catervas closed by itself `purchase_order_expired`, a received or closed one `purchase_order_ended`, and a note over 600 characters `purchase_order_note_too_long`. Only the daemon's token on `POST /command` and the browser's session on its command carry the decision, under `ask` and `auto` alike (ADR 0041): no agent's session reaches them, and `purchase_order.approved` and `rejected` count only from an event whose envelope names no agent and no session. An order not decided within 30 days of its drafting is closed by Catervas (6.10).

Messages to sellers and their replies (added in 0.77; ADR 0039; phase 7 step 10f). A message the Procurement Specialist drafted (`seller_message.drafted`, 6.10) and a reply from a seller (`seller_reply.received`) wait for the owner in Today's own sections, "Messages to sellers" and "Replies from sellers", and are neither a question nor an escalation nor a row of `waiting.list`, so the board marks no task "Waiting on you" for them and `team.activity` says nothing of them. An order's message is not in the first section: it is the `send` of its order's row, which gains `{ message, to, domain, new_domain, subject, body }` while its message waits, and Today offers "Approve and send to <seller>" beside "Approve, I'll place it myself" when the mailbox is connected and fewer than 50 messages have gone today.

A data pipeline that waits for the owner (added in 0.76; phase 7 step 10e). A request the Product Manager escalates with `catervas_decide_data_pipeline`, or that Catervas escalates after three decision sessions that ended without one (6.10), waits for the owner as a purchase order does: it is neither a question nor an escalation of a task and it holds no task. The task keeps its status and its assignee may hand it in, and no task projection counts the request. `waiting.list` gives one row of kind `data_pipeline` for each escalated request not yet decided, after the purchase orders, with `task_id`, `agent_id`, `title` (its task's) and `line`, which reads "<agent name> asks for a data source: <name>", and `pipeline` (its number), `name`, `what`, `url`, `host` (the address's site in its ASCII form), `why`, `cost`, `needs_account`, `sends_project_data`, `reason` (the Product Manager's, absent when Catervas escalated it), `at` and `request_text` (the text the request would be filed with). The agent's activity line reads "Waiting on you: <line>", and a run's end lists it with `catervas pipeline approve <n>, or catervas pipeline decline <n>`. The owner decides with `data_pipeline_decide { pipeline, decision: "approve" | "decline", note? }` (changed in 0.76 after review), a note of at most 600 characters (`pipeline_note_too_long`), refused `pipeline_not_escalated` for a request the Product Manager has not passed on, `pipeline_decided` for one decided and `unknown_pipeline` for a number that is none; the decision names no agent and no session, and approving files the request (5.16).

A marketing plan's ads and their budget (added in 0.72; ADR 0042; phase 7 step 08g). What Catervas did, or could not do, with a plan's ads is not an escalation and holds no task. `waiting.list` gives one row for each plan, ahead of every other row since it is the owner's money, with `task_id` the plan's proposing task, `agent_id` its proposer, `plan`, `plan_title`, `ends_on`, `currency` and amounts as decimal strings; the page words each one from these fields, with Catervas's picture, the plan's title and a campaign's name as text. By precedence, one kind for a plan: `marketing_budget`, for the active plan with a `marketing_budget.reached` recorded, until the plan ends, titled "Ads budget reached: <plan>", with `google_ads` (the plan's Google Ads budget), `spent` (its spend as last read, else as its caps recorded it), `read_at` (when it was read, when it was), `cap` (the budget the row's words are about: the newest, or, when an older cap's pause was refused and a campaign of it has no pause recorded since, that one, with `reason`, Google's words or Catervas's own when no sign-in could be used), `caps` (every budget reached, oldest first, each `{ scope, key?, name?, spent, budget }`), `campaigns` (each of the plan's, `{ key, name, budget, spent }`) and `raising` (the task of the owner's open request for a new version); it says "Its ads reached their budget: <spent> of <budget> <currency>. Catervas paused them." ("Its campaign <name> reached its budget: … Catervas paused it." at a campaign's), or, while a campaign of that cap is unpaused, "… Catervas could not pause them: <reason> Catervas tries again every 15 minutes; pause them in Google Ads.", with the facts "Google Ads: <spent> of <google_ads> <currency> spent" and "Ends <ends_on>", and offers "Raise the budget" (not while a raise is open, when the line adds "You asked <agent> for a new version with a raised budget. It waits for you here when it is ready."), "End the plan" (the End question of the plan's page, 6.5) and, when Catervas could not pause, "Open Google Ads" (ads.google.com, in a new tab). `marketing_ads_running`, for a plan whose ads Catervas could not pause when it ended or when Google Ads was removed (a plan with a `marketing_budget` row has it folded into that row), titled "Ads still running: <plan>": "Catervas could not pause its ads: <reason> They keep running at Google until <ends_on> or their budget there. Pause them in Google Ads." `marketing_spend_unread`, for the active plan whose last read failed, titled "Can't read the ad spend: <plan>": "Catervas can't read its ad spend: <reason> Any of its ads still running keep running at Google until <ends_on> or their budget there; pause them in Google Ads.", with "Last read <when>: <spent> of <google_ads> <currency>" once a read worked. The last two offer "Open Google Ads" and nothing to decide. The reason is Google's words as `Google answered “<words>”`, or Catervas's own sentence when no sign-in could be used ("<agent>'s sign-in to Google has ended; sign <agent> in again on <agent>'s page", "no Marketing Specialist on the team has Google Ads connected"), cut at 300 characters. What the watch read and could not pause is kept in memory, so a restart forgets it, but not the warning: an ended plan's pause is tried again at the watch's first wake and the active plan's read fails while no connection is left, so one of the rows says it again within a minute. A row of a kind the page does not know is skipped.

The user answers, approves, accepts, and resolves from any terminal, while a process drives the project or not (section 3, ADR 0014), and from the web and desktop apps. Every approval and acceptance the user is asked for shows the plain-language summary written by the agent that asks, the diff, and Catervas's check results, as 5.4 says of a human gate (added in 0.18). While the team is paused because the provider refused the key (5.5), Today's list of what waits on the user starts with one row, "Your AI account's key did not work", which links to Settings to connect the account again (added in 0.30). A design review that cannot start (5.4) is listed there too (added in 0.33): with no preview set, "Tell Catervas how to open your app", which opens Settings at "How to open your app"; and while the Designer cannot open the app, "<name> needs Docker's sandbox", with the words "The UI/UX Designer needs Docker's sandbox to open your app. Turn the sandbox on, or retire the Designer.", which opens the Team page; and while the Designer has its Playwright connector off, "<name>'s browser is off", with the words "<name> opens your app with Playwright. Turn Playwright on for <name> on the Team page; until then <name> takes no work, and nobody checks the screens <Developer> builds.", which opens that Designer's page on the Team page (`designer_needs_browser`, added in the step's fix wave). A connector's call waiting for the human is listed there too (added in 0.39): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `tool_approval` carrying `approval`, `server`, `tool` and the whole `input`, which reads "<agent name> wants to use <server>", then "To <tool, its underscores as spaces>, for <task> <title>. <agent name> waits until you decide.", and offers "Review", which opens `ToolApproval` (F9). A post outside the plan waiting for the owner is listed there too (added in 0.57): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `social_post` carrying `post`, `channel`, `text`, `media` and `at`, which reads "<agent name> wants to post on <Network>", after the marketing plans, and Today shows it with the post's words and pictures, "Post it" and "Don't post"; the agent's activity line is "Waiting on you: may <agent name> post on <Network>?". A site a Procurement Specialist asks to read is listed there too (added in 0.71): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `site_request` carrying `request`, `host`, `url` and `why`, which reads "<agent name> asks to read <host>", after the posts outside the plan, and Today shows it with the site, the page as text and never a link, the reason as the agent's own words in a frame that says so, a warning for a name written in another alphabet, and "Allow" and "Don't allow", each asking for an optional note. A purchase order the Procurement Specialist drafted is listed there too (added in 0.74): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `purchase_order`, after the site requests, which reads "<agent name> set up an order from <seller>: <total> <currency>", and Today shows it as the paragraph on a purchase order above says. A marketing plan waiting for the owner is listed there too (added in 0.55): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `marketing_plan` carrying `plan`, `summary`, `total`, `currency`, `starts_on` and `ends_on`, which reads "<agent name> proposes a marketing plan: <title>", the plan's own title, after the connector calls and before the approvals, and offers "Review", which opens the plan's page (6.5). When a process driving the project ends, it says what waits on the user, in this order and by task: each unanswered question with the command that answers it; each connector call waiting for the human, "<task> waits: <agent name> wants to use <server>: catervas tool approve <n>, or catervas tool refuse <n>" and, on the next line, "  input: <the call's whole input>" (added in 0.39; the input line with `--json` is an `input` field); each marketing plan waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> proposes a marketing plan: <title>: catervas marketing plan approve <plan>, or catervas marketing plan return <plan> --reason <text>", with `--json` a `plan` field (added in 0.55); each post outside the plan waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> wants to post on <Network>: catervas marketing post send <n>, or catervas marketing post decline <n>", with `--json` a `post` field (added in 0.57); each site waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> asks to read <host>: catervas site approve <n>, or catervas site decline <n>", with `--json` a `request` field (added in 0.71); each purchase order waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> set up an order from <seller>: <total> <currency>: catervas order approve <n>, or catervas order reject <n>", with `--json` an `order` field (added in 0.74); each contract awaiting approval; each other escalation with its last reason; each `verifying` epic or `high` risk task, which may need the user's acceptance; and, under the `manual` policy, each accepted task waiting to be integrated (added in 0.8). Then, when the team plans its work in sprints, no sprint is open and the Backlog holds work, it prints "start a sprint: <n> waits in the Backlog (`catervas sprint start`)", `<n>` the Backlog's rows with no parent, so an epic counts once, and with `--json` `{"backlog": {"count": <n>}}` (section 3; added in 0.36). Work waiting in the Backlog is not something that waits on the user, so `waiting.list`, Today's "Waiting on you", has no row for it; Today counts it in the team band instead (F3). Nor is it blocked: a Backlog row keeps its status, so the blocked-age rule never counts the wait, escalation aging reads only escalated rows, the escalation digest is unchanged, and nothing escalates because no sprint was started (added in 0.36).

### 5.8 Memory

Three memories, all files in `.catervas/`. Files under `.catervas/` change only through Catervas's tools, never through a task's commit: the Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose allowed paths reach there (5.3) and the Definition of Done a diff that changes a path there (5.4), for every role, the Finance Specialist's own `.catervas/local/finance/` excepted (6.6; changed in 0.21) (added in 0.17).

- `decisions/` holds architecture decision records and product decisions, written by the Architect and PM, one file each, immutable once written (changed in 0.16, from "once accepted": nothing accepts a decision). A decision is written only through `catervas_write_decision { title, text }`, a tool of the read tier that refuses anyone but the Architect and the Product Manager with `decision_refused: only the Architect and the Product Manager write decisions`. Its title is 1 to 120 characters once trimmed, on one line with no control characters, and its text 1 to 32,000 characters and not blank; anything else is refused with `decision_refused`. It is numbered one past the highest number among the decision files, in four digits, and a project that has 9999 is refused. Its file is `decisions/<NNNN>-<slug>.md`. The slug is the title in lower case, with each run of characters other than `a-z` and `0-9` turned into one `-`, trimmed of `-`, cut at 60 characters and trimmed again, or `decision` when nothing is left. The file holds `# <NNNN>. <title>`, a blank line, `Date: <UTC date>`, `By: <agent id>`, a blank line, and then the text. The file is written under a temporary name and then hard-linked to its own name, which fails when that name exists, so a crash never leaves a half-written decision and no decision is written over. When another writer took the number first, whatever title it gave, the tool tries the next number, three times at most, and then refuses. No tool edits a decision. Each decision is recorded as `decision.written`. Every agent reads the decisions through `catervas_read_decisions { number? }`, a tool of the read tier offered wherever `catervas_read_rules` is. With no number it answers every decision's number, title, date, and author, oldest first; with a number it answers that decision's whole file; an unknown number is refused with `no_such_decision: <n>`. It is a tool rather than the file tools because the decisions live in the project's own checkout, which a task's worktree is not (5.14). A file named `NNNN-<slug>.md` in that form and written by hand is listed too: its title comes from its `# ` heading, else its slug, and its date and author from its `Date:` and `By:` lines when it has them. Any other file there is ignored (added in 0.16).
- `agents/<name>/memory.md` is the agent's own notebook. It is included in every session for that agent. The agent may edit it. It is capped at a size (default 8k tokens) and the agent is told to prune. The agent writes it only through `catervas_write_memory { text }`, which replaces the whole notebook; an empty text empties it. The tool is of the read tier and offered in every session but the two given one tool alone, triage and the judgment (added in 0.16). The cap is the team policy `memory_cap_tokens`, from 500 to 16,000, default 8,000. A text's tokens are a quarter of its characters, rounded up, which is also how the channel summary is measured (5.9). A text past the cap is refused with `memory_refused: <n> tokens is past your cap of <cap>; prune it`, and nothing is written or recorded. A write is recorded as `memory.written` with the whole text (added in 0.16). Every session's prompt says how full the notebook is, as `<n> of <cap> tokens.`, a line Catervas writes outside the notebook's `untrusted` block. When `n` is past 80 percent of the cap and the session can call `catervas_write_memory`, the line goes on: `Prune it with catervas_write_memory before it reaches the cap.` The prompt cuts the notebook at four characters for each token of the cap, never splitting a character, so a notebook within its cap is never cut (8.2; added in 0.16).
- `team/retro.md` accumulates retrospective learnings. Appended after each sprint by whoever runs the retro ceremony: the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager on a team with no active one (changed in 0.17). Included in every planning session. It is written only by the retro ceremony (5.9), with `catervas_append_retro`, once per sprint whichever session asks: a text that is not blank and holds at most 4,000 characters, appended under a heading `## <sprint id> (<date>)`, the ended sprint's id and the UTC date of the append, in a file Catervas starts with a `# Retro` title when it is missing, and recorded as `retro.appended`. Anywhere else the tool is refused with `retro_refused`. The planning and retro ceremonies are given its last 16 KiB (added in 0.15).

The project scan from onboarding is stored as `project.md` and refreshed after an integration when what it reads has changed (changed in 0.16, from "whenever the tree changes materially"). The refresh runs once for each integration that records `task.integrated`: Catervas's merge, the human's `catervas integrate`, a pull request merged on the forge, and a branch the human merged by hand (5.14). A task that was already integrated is not scanned again. The scan reads the working tree at the project's root, so the refresh runs only when the project's own checkout is on the integration branch; on another branch, detached, or unreadable, it is skipped, and the reason is given. It compares the new scan with the last `project.scanned`: the read-back without its closing part about the last commit (`last commit ...` or `no commits yet`), and the names of the criteria found. A new commit alone is no change, and neither is a detected command that changes under the same name. A project with no `project.scanned` counts as changed. When the two differ, Catervas rewrites `project.md`, reseeds the criterion library as `catervas init` does (5.13), writing it and recording `criteria.updated` by `governor` when it changed, and records `project.scanned` last. Each event is about the integrated task, and no model is involved. A refresh that fails never fails the integration, though what it wrote before the failure stays written; since it recorded no `project.scanned`, the next integration does it again. The integration's own words then end with "; the project scan was refreshed", with nothing when it was unchanged, or with "; the project scan was not refreshed: <why>", in `catervas run`'s line and in `catervas integrate`'s answer; so do the words of an escalation that follows a merge, such as a failed push (added in 0.16).

There is no vector store in the first release. Grep over the repository and these files is enough for repositories of the size the target users have. This is a known limit to revisit.

### 5.9 The channel

Agents post to the channel in a conversational register. The persona line and a shared style guide control tone. What triggers a post:

- State transitions on tasks the agent owns or reviews (assigned, done, blocked, rejected with the one-line reason).
- Mentions from other agents or the user.
- Planning, standup, review, and retro ceremonies, which are structured conversations that run in the channel.
- A configurable "ambient" allowance: a small number of unprompted messages per sprint per agent, so the team feels alive without becoming expensive. Default is one, and reactions are one or two sentences (decided 2026-09-15: keep the register minimal).

A message (added in 0.14) is a `message.posted` event, and the log is the channel's only storage. It has an author, a kind, one line of text, the agents it mentions, a ceremony thread when it belongs to one, and the message it answers when it is a reply. The author is an agent, `human`, or `catervas`. The text is not blank and holds at most 2,000 characters, and Catervas turns each line break in it into a space. There are six kinds:

- `reaction`: an agent's first post in a session that is not a conversation, about the move that session made.
- `ambient`: each later post of such a session, from the agent's allowance.
- `reply`: the one post of a conversation session.
- `ceremony`: a post of a ceremony session, in its thread (changed in 0.15).
- `system`: Catervas's line, written with no model.
- `human`: the human's post, from `catervas say`.

Mentions are read by Catervas from the text, never taken from the agent: each `@<id>` naming an agent of the team, active or not, once each, leaving out the author. `@human` is not a mention, because the human reads the channel. A system line mentions no one.

An agent posts with `catervas_post_message`, tier `read`, which every session has except a one-tool session (triage, the judgment; changed in 0.15: sprint planning is now a ceremony, which has it) and a one-to-one chat's session (added in 0.34). Catervas decides the kind from the session's purpose, and a refusal says `channel_limit`. The tool's description is the style guide: one or two sentences, what happened and what is next, no instruction to anyone.

The reaction comes from the session that made the move, on that session's model (changed in 0.14; this replaces "ambient and reaction messages use a cheaper model than task work"). The closing instruction of `refine`, `plan`, `implement`, and `verify` sessions asks for it: after asking for a move, post one or two sentences about it in the persona's voice, naming the task. No session is started only to react. A reaction's and an ambient message's envelope names the session's task.

Catervas posts a system line with no model:

- for a move made by the governor or the human, which has no session;
- for the rejection Catervas files in a reviewer's name once the review session has ended;
- when the daily budget or a sprint's budget is spent;
- when an agent sleeps at its model provider's limit (5.5).

A move's line reads `<task> <from> → <to> (by <who>)`, followed by `: <reason>` when the move carries one: the escalation's detail, the failed criteria and the reviewer's reasons for a rejection, the blocker, the resolution, the human's reason, or the criterion Catervas could not run. It is cut to 2,000 characters ending in `…`, so a line never fails a move that is recorded. It is posted after the move's own events. The moves Catervas makes on an agent's behalf post nothing, which keeps the register minimal: a triaged draft to `refining`, an assigned task to `in_progress`, an epic's assignment.

The ambient allowance is the team policy `ambient_messages_per_sprint`, from 0 to 20, default 1 (added in 0.14). It counts the agent's `ambient` messages since the open sprint's `sprint.started`, or since the UTC day began when no sprint is open. A post past it is refused.

A mention gets its agent one short conversation session (added in 0.14). A mention is pending while it is newer than the latest message the agent's last conversation session was shown, which that session's `session.started` records as `in_reply_to`, so that a message posted while the session starts stays pending. A conversation that ends any way but at its provider's limit uses up the mentions it was shown; one stopped at the limit leaves them pending until its agent wakes. Mentions in a `reply`, a system line, or a `ceremony` message are never pending, so replies are one deep and a ceremony's words start no conversation (changed in 0.15). In `catervas run`, not `catervas plan`, after the budget rule and before the third rule, and when a tick is scoped to no task, the orchestrator takes the first agent in team order with a pending mention that is active and not asleep, while the daily budget has room. It starts one `conversation` session about no task, on Claude Sonnet 5.5 at `low` effort whatever the agent's own model. Its first message lists the pending messages with their authors and sequence numbers, then the channel summary, each in an `untrusted` block. Its closing instruction asks it to answer the mentions once with `catervas_post_message` and to file any work as a request with `catervas_create_task` without a parent. Its one post is a `reply` to the latest pending message, and a second is refused. `catervas run` prints it as `<agent>: <what>`. A mention of an agent that is paused, retired, or asleep waits.

The ceremonies (added in 0.15). Planning, standup, review, and retro are each one session of the ceremony runner: the active Scrum Master, else the active Product Manager, and with neither no ceremony runs. The session's purpose is `ceremony`, it is about no task, and it runs on Claude Sonnet 5.5 at `medium` effort whatever the runner's own model, with the read tier's built-in tools alone. Its `session.started` records its thread, `planning`, `standup`, `review`, or `retro`, and each of its posts is a `ceremony` message in that thread, at most three per session: a fourth is refused with `channel_limit`. Its Catervas tools are the four reading tools, `catervas_post_message`, `catervas_write_memory`, and `catervas_read_decisions` (the last two added in 0.16); planning adds `catervas_plan_sprint`, and retro adds `catervas_append_retro`. `catervas_plan_sprint` is refused in any other session, whichever tools it lists, with `sprint_plan_refused: only the planning ceremony plans a sprint` (added in 0.17). Its first message gives it the ceremony's facts and then the channel summary, each in an `untrusted` block cut at 16 KiB, and its closing instruction says what to post, which tool ends it, and to mention no one, because the channel is read by all. No ceremony starts on a day whose budget is spent, and one whose runner sleeps at its provider's limit waits for it rather than passing to the other role.

A ceremony has run once a session in its thread started after its anchor and ended completed, aborted, or in error, or once three such sessions started, however they ended, so a ceremony stopped at a limit is tried again and none is tried forever; a retro has also run once it has appended, however its session ended, so no sprint gets two retros. The anchor is the sprint's `sprint.started` for planning, its `sprint.ended` for review and retro, and the start of the UTC day for a standup. A task-less `plan` session with no thread, from a log recorded before this revision, counts as the sprint's planning. The ceremonies run in a tick about no one task, before the rules of work and after the rule that ends a sprint, in the order review, retro, planning, standup:

- Planning runs once when a sprint starts, in `catervas run`, `catervas serve`, and `catervas plan`. It is given the ready backlog, the sprint's budget left, the escalation digest (5.7), and the last 16 KiB of `team/retro.md`. It posts the plan and the digest, then plans the sprint with `catervas_plan_sprint`. When the team plans its work in sprints (section 3; added in 0.36), its candidates are the Backlog's rows with no parent, ready tasks and epics that are ready, being broken down or broken down, the message naming them "each waiting in the Backlog" and giving each epic the number of tasks under it, its cancelled ones left out; `catervas_plan_sprint` then accepts a Backlog row with no parent, a task under way since the switch not being one, and refuses another with "<task> is <status>, and only work waiting in the Backlog is planned". With the policy off, the candidates stay the `ready` rows with no parent and in no sprint, and the tool accepts only a ready task or an approved epic.
- Standup runs in `catervas run` and `catervas serve`, while a sprint is open, once per UTC day, when a task of the sprint moved in its window: from the start of the UTC day of the last standup that has run, or from the sprint's start, up to the start of the current UTC day. So a standup reports the days before today, and a move made after midnight but before that day's standup is reported the next day. It is given each move (task, from, to, and who), each blocked task of the sprint with its blocker, and the open escalations, and posts one summary.
- Review and retro run in `catervas run` and `catervas serve` for the latest ended sprint while no newer sprint has started; a sprint followed at once by another gets neither, and so does one whose file lists no task. Their tasks are those the sprint's file lists, and their events those about those tasks recorded between the sprint's start and its end. Review comes first: it is given each task's status, the first line of its last completion note, and what it cost in the sprint, then the sprint's budget and what it spent, and it posts what the sprint delivered and what it did not. Retro follows once review has run: it is given the sprint's rejections with their failed criteria, escalations with their reason, blocks with their blocker, each task's iterations, and the last 16 KiB of `team/retro.md`, and it posts the retro and records what the next planning should know with `catervas_append_retro` (5.8).

`catervas run` prints a ceremony as `S<n>: <what>`.

The channel summary is derived with no model (changed in 0.14): the most recent messages that fit 2,000 tokens, counted as a quarter of their characters rounded up, oldest first, one line each as `<author> [<thread>]: <text>`. It is written to `.catervas/local/channel-summary.md` each time it is made, so the human can read what an agent was shown.

One-to-one chats (added in 0.34; ADR 0026) are not the channel. A chat message is a `chat_message.posted` event (8.5), never a `message.posted`, so no reader of the channel reads one: not `channel.messages`, the channel summary, `catervas channel`, the channel's pending mentions, nor `catervas metrics`' count of messages; an `@<id>` in a chat starts no conversation, and no session is shown a chat but its own agent's chat session. The user's message and the agent's reply are 1 to 4,000 characters, counted in code points, not blank, with their line breaks kept; a proposed request's title is 1 to 120 characters on one line and its text 20 to 4,000. A chat is pending when the sequence number of the user's newest message in it is above the `in_reply_to` of every `chat` session its agent has started, whoever wrote last, so a message sent while a session runs is answered next, with the reply in its history, and a session that ended without a reply is not tried again until the user writes again. In `catervas run` and `catervas serve`, not `catervas plan`, as the first rule of a tick scoped to no task, before the sprint rules, the orchestrator takes the oldest pending chat, by that message's sequence number, of an agent that is not retired (a paused agent among them), is not asleep, and whose day's budget has room, and starts one `chat` session for it (8.2), whose `in_reply_to` is that message; `catervas run` prints it as `<agent>: <what>`, as a conversation's. While the team is paused the tick runs this rule alone, and none while the pause is Catervas's for a key the provider refused (5.5). A command that sends a chat message wakes a waiting `catervas serve`. The privacy of chats rests on `.catervas/local/**` staying protected (5.6): the log, `.catervas/local/catervas.db`, lives there, and a chat session's `Read`, `Grep` and `Glob` run in the project's root, so a user who takes that glob out of the protected list lets every session read every chat.

The hard rule again: nothing said in the channel creates work. An agent that wants something done files a request, which is triaged and contracted like a request from the user (5.16). The tool for filing a request is available in the channel session precisely so that the conversational path leads into the governed path rather than around it.

### 5.10 What the harness does not solve

Two honest gaps. First, verification quality is only as good as the exit criteria the PM writes. A vague criterion passed by a sloppy reviewer is still a pass. The Scrum Master's judgment check and the human review of `high` risk contracts are the mitigations; they are not proofs. Second, the governor can prevent forbidden actions but cannot detect a semantically wrong change that stays within its allowed paths and passes its tests. Catervas makes that kind of error cheap to find (small tasks, diffs per task, a reviewer who reads them) rather than impossible.

### 5.11 Contract ownership (added in 0.2)

Every contract has an owner. A contract the Product Manager wrote is owned by the team until the human locks it. A contract with `locked: true` is owned by the human: agents may record criterion results and write their notes on it, and the governor refuses every other change by an agent, with the reason `contract_locked` when the change is to the contract's content and the reason for the field's owner when the field is one that agent never writes anyway. A lock holds the contract's content for the human; it does not stop the lifecycle, so the governor still writes `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, and `sprint` when it applies a transition, or a locked task could never leave `refining`. Neither locking nor unlocking is a content change: a human who locks a contract from `ready` onward does not send its task back to `refining`, or writing an epic by hand and locking it would undo the approval it was just given (5.16). Locking and unlocking are human commands, recorded as `contract.locked` and `contract.unlocked` events. This is how a user writes a contract themselves, or with the Product Manager's help (5.13), and has the team execute exactly that contract.

The contract's `summary` (added in 0.25) is a content field, the Product Manager's to write like the others, and so frozen with them once the task leaves `refining`. Every field of a contract has exactly one owner, and the governor refuses a write to a field the writer does not own (added in 0.3). The governor's own are `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, and `sprint`, at every status and whoever asks: 5.2 gives it the `status` field for its transitions, and every other row's trigger is an actor asking rather than writing, so a write of `status` by anyone else would move a task past every gate with no `task.transitioned` event behind it. The governor writes `sprint` when a task is planned into the open sprint, by the assigner's plan or when it is filed under an epic in that sprint, and clears it when the task leaves a sprint the human ended before the task was accepted or cancelled; an accepted or cancelled task keeps the sprint it finished in (added in 0.12). `locked` is the human's alone. The store's are `id`, `created_by`, `created_at`, and `updated_at`, which no actor writes through a contract tool. `kind` and `parent` are fixed when the contract is created: the triage decides the kind (5.16) and its own tool changes it, and clearing `parent` would take a task out of the epic its readiness was judged against. The notes are everyone's, at every status. Everything else is the contract's content, `change` among it (added in 0.11), written by the Product Manager and by the human on any contract, and by the Scrum Master on a task but never on an epic (6.2, 5.16 item 3); an assignee, a reviewer, and the governor never write content at all. A field name the governor does not recognise is refused as unknown rather than treated as content, so a field added to the schema is refused until somebody says who owns it. A contract whose task is `accepted` or `cancelled` takes no write but a note, the human's included: nothing leaves those two statuses, and a human's write of a frozen contract is defined by sending the task back to `refining`, which a terminal task has no way to do.

Independently of ownership, a contract is frozen once its task leaves `refining`: from `ready` onward, only `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, `sprint`, and `notes` change, the sprint because a task is put into one at planning and planning works from the ready backlog, and only through governed transitions and the note tools. An agent that wants a frozen contract changed asks the human (5.7). A human who edits a frozen contract moves the task back to `refining`, and the log says so. From the web UI that edit is saved on an escalated task, written while the task is still held, and only then does the escalation's resolve take it back, so that the session the move wakes reads the human's plan (changed in 0.26 by step 08's landing review); a save of a frozen contract the team is still working to is refused, and the user escalates the task first (added in 0.25's landing review). The human's save of a contract from the web UI never changes `locked`: a save whose `locked` differs from the file's is refused, because a lock is recorded as `contract.locked` or `contract.unlocked`, which a save does not write (added in 0.25's landing review).

### 5.12 Team rules (added in 0.2)

Team rules are constraints the human writes once, in `.catervas/team.yaml` under `rules`, that the governor applies to every contract and every tool call. Agents read them in every session and cannot change them.

| Rule | Type | Enforced where |
|---|---|---|
| `protected_paths` | globs | every file tool call, read or write, is refused inside them (5.6) |
| `allowed_paths_ceiling` | globs | Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose `allowed_paths` reach outside them. A path lies within a directory glob such as `docs/**` only when its literal part, up to its first wildcard, reaches past the directory's `/` (`docs/adr/**`, `docs/*.md`), or it has no wildcard and is the directory or below it; `docs*/**` and `docs?/x` are outside, since the wildcard runs on into a sibling such as `docsrc/` (changed in 0.11); a path with a `..` segment anywhere, before or after a wildcard, is within no ceiling (changed in 0.17) |
| `required_criteria` | verification methods | Definition of Ready refuses a contract that has no criterion of each listed method |
| `require_new_tests` | boolean | Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose `test` criteria do not set `new_tests_required` |
| `max_task_budget_usd` | number | Definition of Ready refuses a task whose budget exceeds it, when it is set; an epic is bounded by the sprint budget instead |
| `document_paths` | globs | Definition of Ready refuses a task assigned to any role but the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer whose `allowed_paths` reach outside them (5.3; added in 0.11; the Designer added in 0.31) |
| `ui_paths` | globs | a Software Developer's task whose branch changes a path inside them is a change to the interface, which the UI/UX Designer checks before the Architect reviews it (5.4; added in 0.33) |
| `forbidden_commands` | regular expressions | `catervas_exec` refuses a command that matches one: ECMAScript patterns, which have no inline flags such as `(?i)`, matched against the whole command and against each non-blank segment (split on `&&`, `||`, `;`, `\|`, and newlines), and a pattern that does not compile refuses every command |

Defaults: `protected_paths` as in 5.6, `max_task_budget_usd` unset, no cap (ADR 0015), `document_paths` `docs/**`, `**/*.md`, and `CHANGELOG.md` when the key is left out, `ui_paths` `**/*.tsx`, `**/*.jsx`, `**/*.vue`, `**/*.svelte`, `**/*.css`, `**/*.scss`, and `**/*.html` when the key is left out, where an explicit `[]` is kept and leaves only the contract's `ui_change` (added in 0.33), everything else empty or off. The web app edits `ui_paths` under Advanced, as "Which files are screens" (added in 0.33). An explicit `document_paths: []` is kept, not replaced by the default, and means that no task but a Software Developer's or a UI/UX Designer's can be ready (added in 0.11; the Designer added in 0.31). Rules never loosen a permission tier; they only narrow what a granted tier allows.

Beside the rules, `team.yaml`'s `policy` holds the team's policies, flat keys such as `wip_limit_per_agent`, `escalation_age_hours` and `ambient_messages_per_sprint`. `plan_in_sprints` is one of them (added in 0.36): a boolean, read as `false` when the key is left out, which setup and `catervas init` write as `true`, and which section 3 says what it holds. It is a policy, not a rule: the user switches it in Settings (4.4), and it narrows when work is assigned, not what a tool call may do.

### 5.13 Contract authoring and the criterion library (added in 0.2)

A contract can be written three ways, and all three end in the same Definition of Ready check. The human writes it alone, in the editor or as a YAML file, and then the human's own triage sets whether it is an epic or a single task (5.16). The Product Manager writes it from a brief, an issue link, or the backlog, as in 6.1. Or the two write it together: the Product Manager drafts, the human edits, the Definition of Ready results update as they type, and the human locks the result (5.11). The command line offers the same through `catervas contract new`, which files the request, runs its triage (or takes the user's), then runs a Product Manager drafting session over a brief or a link, answering its questions at the terminal, and prints the contract with its readiness results.

`catervas contract new --brief <text>` or `--from <issue>` (added in 0.8). The request's title is the brief's first line, cut at 120 characters, or the issue's title; its intent is the whole brief, or the issue's title, body, and address, and a brief under twenty characters, the schema's least intent, is refused before anything is filed. Every other field is a placeholder the Product Manager replaces while refining it: one item in scope and one out, a requirement, a `review` criterion, both roles Software Developer, risk `low`, the team's cap on a task's budget, and an allowed path no file matches. When no other process drives the project, this one starts driving it before anything is filed (8.2) and holds the run lock throughout, so that a start that refuses, for want of a credential or of a recent enough `claude`, files nothing. The request is then filed as the human's, sized when `--size` is given, and worked, in this process, with the triage and refining rules alone and for that request alone (8.2): each event about it is printed as `catervas task show` prints it, and each question the Product Manager asks is printed with an `answer>` prompt, whose reply is recorded as the answer. The end of the input or Ctrl-C at the prompt leaves the question open for `catervas answer`. Once nothing is left to do it prints the contract, its readiness (passed; failed with each failure; passed the structural checks, when it waits for the user's approval; or not judged yet, and why), what waits on the user, and, with `--lock`, takes the contract, last, so that the Product Manager writes first; when the Product Manager has written none, it says so and takes nothing. While another process drives the project, the request is filed and sized and left to that process, and `--lock` is refused before anything is filed.

The criterion library, `.catervas/team/criteria.yaml`, holds named, reusable exit criteria: the project's own check and test commands found by the project scan, and any the human adds. When authoring, a criterion is referenced by name and expanded into the contract, so that contracts across a project verify the same way and the Product Manager is not asked to reinvent "the tests pass" every time.

### 5.14 Integration of accepted work (added in 0.2)

Each task works on its own branch in its own git worktree under `.catervas/local/worktrees/CTV-<n>`, so that tasks running in parallel never share a working tree. The container for a task mounts that worktree. The branch says what the task changes (changed in 0.11): a Software Developer's or a UI/UX Designer's task (the Designer added in 0.31) works on `feature/CTV-<n>`, or on `fix/CTV-<n>` when its contract's `change` is `fix` (an absent `change` reads as `feature`), and every other role's task on `docs/CTV-<n>`. The name is derived from the contract whenever it is needed, not recorded, since `assignee_role` and `change` are frozen once the task leaves `refining` (5.11). A branch `catervas/CTV-<n>` made before 0.11 is neither renamed nor read. A repository with a branch named exactly `feature`, `fix`, or `docs` cannot hold a branch under that name, so git refuses the task's worktree at assignment and the refusal reaches the user as any git failure does; renaming that branch fixes it.

What happens to the branch after `accepted` is the team's `integration` policy: `auto_merge` (the default a new project gets, named `local_merge` until 0.8: Catervas merges the task branch into the integration branch with a merge commit and pushes the integration branch to `origin` when the repository has one), `manual` (Catervas does nothing until the human runs `catervas integrate`, which merges locally and does not push), or `pull_request` (Catervas pushes the branch and opens a pull request with the `gh` program, titled with the task's id and title, whose body is the contract's intent, the completion note, and the review note; every such pull request needs the human's approval and merge on the forge, and the task is integrated when it merges, the local integration branch then brought up to the forge's). Under `pull_request` Catervas reads each open pull request's state once a tick; one closed without merging is an integration escalation, and the human's `catervas integrate` then records it as integrated if the branch is in the integration branch all the same (merged by hand, or through another pull request) and otherwise says to reopen it. `gh` picks the base repository itself, which in a fork with an `upstream` remote can be `upstream`; `gh repo set-default` settles it. Catervas's pushes are its own act on the host, with the user's own git credentials, under the policy the user chose; no agent is acting, so no agent's `git_remote` is asked (ADR 0012; added in 0.8). A task is awaiting integration on the board from its move into `accepted` until `task.integrated` records where its branch landed; an epic, whose children carry the branches, never is. The integration branch is the repository's default branch unless the team configures another; the repository's default branch is what `origin/HEAD` points at, and a repository with no remote records one nowhere, so there it is the branch that is checked out (added in 0.5).

A task in a private folder, a Finance Specialist's or a Procurement Specialist's (0.70), has no branch (6.6; added in 0.21; as built in 0.62). For it, `accepted` is the end: its move into `accepted` carries the effect `nothing_to_integrate`, so the board never shows it awaiting integration, no `task.integrated` is recorded, the integration policy does not apply, and the human's `catervas integrate` of it is refused `nothing_to_integrate`; a task that depends on it counts it integrated, so its dependants may be assigned (5.2). It is not assigned while another task holds the folder (5.2, 6.6), the two roles' folders being apart, so a finance task and a procurement task may run at once.

One task integrates at a time (added in 0.5). A merge moves the repository's own checkout — the integration branch is checked out, merged into, and what was there is put back — so two integrations at once on one repository can interleave and land a merge commit on the wrong branch. Tasks work in their own worktrees and are unaffected; it is integration alone that is serialised. Every merge, push, and pull request opening holds an exclusive lock on `.catervas/local/integration.lock`, a file lock because `catervas integrate` runs in a process of its own beside `catervas run`, and reads the log again under it, so that two callers racing for one task record one integration (added in 0.8).

When integration cannot land (added in 0.8). A conflict, git refusing the merge, or a push that fails raises an escalation with reason `integration` whose detail is git's words; nothing leaves `accepted` (5.2), so the task stays `accepted` and awaiting integration, and the escalation is a message rather than a move. A failed push leaves the merge in place, because dependents start from the local integration branch, and its detail says to run `git push origin <branch>` once it can be pushed. Catervas does not try again after an integration escalation: the human's `catervas integrate` is the way on, and it integrates whatever the escalations, each failed attempt raising its own. A branch the human already merged by hand is "already up to date" to that merge, which records it. No git Catervas runs may prompt: each runs with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` and an empty `GIT_ASKPASS`, so a push or fetch that wants a credential no helper gives fails with git's words. A push or fetch also runs ssh in batch mode (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND="ssh -o BatchMode=yes"`), so that ssh fails rather than asking on the terminal for a host key or a passphrase while the integration lock is held, unless the user chose how git runs ssh (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_SSH`, or `core.sshCommand`), which Catervas leaves as it is. The team's `integration_branch` is checked by git as a branch name before it is used, so a name such as `main:other` or `-f` is refused rather than read as a refspec or an option, and so is a name git reads as another branch (`@`, `@{-1}`).

Who commits (changed in 0.36). A task's commits, made through `catervas_git_commit`, and integration's merge commits are the user's: Catervas commits as the identity git has for the repository. When git has none there, `user.name` or `user.email` empty or unset at the repository's effective scope, as on a fresh computer or a CI runner, Catervas commits as `catervas <catervas@localhost>`, the identity the project setup's first commit is made with, rather than leave git to refuse. A commit that still fails is answered to the agent with git's words, and Catervas also posts a line in the channel about the task saying it could not be committed and why, since a task whose work is not committed never reaches `verifying`.

A task is assigned only when every dependency it lists is accepted and integrated, a Finance Specialist's task counting as integrated once accepted, since it has no branch (6.6; added in 0.21), and its branch starts from the integration branch at that moment. Worktrees and containers are removed when a task is accepted or cancelled; branches are kept. A container that cannot be removed, docker being down, leaves the task's worktrees in place to be removed on a later tick, and holds up no other work.

### 5.15 Recovery (added in 0.2)

Catervas can stop at any moment: the laptop closes, the process is killed. On startup it reconciles the event log with itself and with the files (8.4): every session that has a `session.started` event without a `session.ended` one is ended with reason `aborted` and the detail "interrupted: catervas stopped before the session ended" (the wire has no `interrupted` reason, and nothing would read one differently; changed in 0.8), and one with no `cost.recorded` is recorded as costing nothing on the model its start names, so that it counts against its task's sessions as any session does; every task that was `in_progress` stays `in_progress` and its next session starts from the last commit on its branch and the last note, in a sandbox made afresh, a container left by the killed run being replaced; a `verifying` task gets its reviewer's session again without its recorded criteria being run again; worktrees and containers that belong to accepted or cancelled tasks are removed, the containers by name since no handle to them survived, and the task's own worktree last, so that a recovery stopped part way does the rest next time. Nothing is lost that was committed or logged, and nothing is repeated that was recorded as done.

### 5.16 Epics: from a request to sub-contracts (added in 0.3)

Every prompt from the user that asks for work is translated into a contract before anything else happens, and so is every request an agent files from the channel, and every request the user sends from a one-to-one chat (changed in 0.34: the agent proposes it there, and the user files it, 4.3). The first thing that happens to a request is triage: the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager when the team has no active Scrum Master, decides whether the request is large or small and records the decision with a reason (`request.triaged`). A large request becomes an epic (`kind: epic`). A small request becomes a single standalone task (`kind: task`, no `parent`), written by the Product Manager like any task, with its questions to the user asked first and the user's approval required only by the team's policy (`human_accepts_contracts`, or `high` risk); it then goes through the ordinary lifecycle without a breakdown. The governor refuses `draft → refining` until the triage is recorded, and the user may overrule it (`catervas triage <id> large|small`, or the board) before the Product Manager starts. Triage is a session of the triager's, the Scrum Master's when the team has an active one (changed in 0.10), and runs on the cheaper model, `claude-sonnet-5-5` at low effort whatever the triager's own model (changed in 0.33 from `claude-sonnet-5`), with `catervas_triage_request` as its one tool and no built-in tool, and produces one event, so a small request costs one short session on top of its own. Once a request is triaged, Catervas moves it to `refining` on the Product Manager's behalf with no session; the user's window to overrule the triage is the draft (added in 0.8). A request an approved data pipeline files (6.10) is triaged as any other (added in 0.76).

An epic goes through the same lifecycle as a task with four differences.

1. The Product Manager writes it, and must ask the user every question it needs first. In an epic's `refining` sessions the Product Manager asks through the question mechanism (5.7) before it writes the contract, and it may not write or change product documents (the spec, requirements, or the product roadmap under `.catervas/product/`) until the epic is approved: the `catervas_write_product_doc` tool is refused until the user has approved the contract the epic has now, and for a `cancelled` epic, whose documents would describe a decision the team abandoned (added in 0.3). The governor asks the approval rather than the status, because an epic waiting for approval, one that failed the Definition of Ready three times, and one whose risk needs the human all sit in `escalated`; a return to `refining` ends the approval and item 2 asks for it again, and an epic escalated after its approval, for a budget or a permission, keeps the documents it was approved for. When the Product Manager believes it has no questions, it says so in the contract's intent, and the user's approval is the check that it was right.
2. The user reads and approves it, from the Product Manager's plain-language summary for the human, the contract in full, and its Definition of Ready results (5.4; added in 0.18). Every epic requires human acceptance of the contract before it leaves `refining`, whatever its risk: once the contract passes the structural checks, the governor moves the epic to `escalated` with reason `approval` and the board shows it as awaiting approval; the user approves, which moves it to `ready`, or sends it back to `refining` with a message that starts the next refining session. Nothing is broken down before this approval. A contract written in `refining`, or one filed whole (a breakdown's task, or a contract the user holds), is judged by the governor before the Product Manager refines it again; one that fails goes back to the Product Manager with its failures (added in 0.8).
3. Its assignee breaks it down. An approved epic is assigned to the Scrum Master, or to the Product Manager when the team has no active Scrum Master, and that assignee's work is to write the epic's tasks (`kind: task`, `parent` set), each with clear deliverables and exit criteria, and to assign them to the appropriate agents (5.2). A task's `allowed_paths` fall within its epic's, its budget within the epic's remaining budget, and its parent must be `in_progress`; the Definition of Ready checks all three. A task under an epic may be created only by its epic's assignee or by the human; a standalone task comes only from triage. Catervas assigns an approved epic on its assignee's behalf, with no session, once that assignee holds fewer open tasks than the WIP limit, and starts it (added in 0.8): to the Scrum Master when the team has an active one, with the Product Manager as its reviewer (added in 0.10), and otherwise to the Product Manager, whose epic the human reviews. Who reviews an epic is fixed by that assignment and read from the epic's row thereafter, the human when the row names no reviewer, so that a Scrum Master activated or paused later changes no assigned epic's reviewer.
